      "text": "Below it, smaller: *Mary Ellery. Beloved.*"
    },
    {
      "line": 17,
      "text": "He had signed the deed at the solicitor’s in June. He remembered the paper, the thick cream envelope, the way the woman had turned it round to face him and said, *the Ellery property*, as though the house were still a person’s."
    },
    {
      "line": 25,
      "text": "At home, the sideboard was out from the wall again."
    },
    {
      "line": 41,
      "text": "“It’s on a runner.” They shoved, and the sideboard went back against the wall with a dull knock, and they dusted their palms on their jeans. “There. Done.”"
    },
    {
      "line": 49,
      "text": "“Dave.” They said his name kindly. That was the worst of it. “You did. You moved it yesterday and forgot, and now you’ve got yourself worked up.” They picked up the coffee and looked at the dust line, and something flickered across their face, there and gone. “It’s an old house. Things settle. You’re tired.”"
    },
    {
      "line": 57,
      "text": "“Something.” They smiled, and it was almost gentle, and it still had that little shine along the rim. “Something crossed the landing, and the chair turned round, and the sideboard’s walked three inches out from the wall, and the windows are dirty on the inside. Do you hear yourself, love?”"
    },
    {
      "line": 65,
      "text": "They laughed. He laughed too, a half-beat late, and the sound sat wrong in his chest. Then they went back to the crossword, and Dave stood in the front room with the sideboard flat to the wall and the dust line still showing pale on the boards where it had been."
    },
    {
      "line": 75,
      "text": "“Dave.”"
    },
    {
      "line": 79,
      "text": "The voice came from halfway up, from the landing where the stair turned, and it was a woman’s voice, low and clear. It said his name once. It said it correctly, without the question his partner put on it now, without the old teasing drawl. Just *Dave*, the way he said it himself inside his own head, the way someone who had known him a long time might say it across a room."
    },
    {
      "line": 89,
      "text": "Dave stood at the bottom of his own stairs and felt the want move through him, hot and shameful and utterly unreasonable: that the voice would come again. Not the shadow, not the knock, not the cold. The voice. That it would say his name once more, and go on saying it, and that he would be heard."
    }
  ]
}

      "text": "The lane narrowed as it went, as though the town had been squeezing the road out of existence for a hundred years and had nearly finished. At the end of it stood the house: two storeys, grey stone, taller than it was wide, with a slate roof that sagged in the middle like a tired back. Dave stood in the wet road with a box in his arms and looked up at the windows."
    },
    {
      "line": 13,
      "text": "The key turned with a sound like a knuckle cracking. The door swung in on a hallway that was longer than the outside of the house had any right to promise, dim despite the hour, papered in something that had once been green. A runner ran down the middle of it, worn to the weave in two dark tracks where generations of feet had gone back and forth."
    },
    {
      "line": 17,
      "text": "It was a pocket of it, a hand’s width of air that had no business being there in September, cold enough that his arms came up in gooseflesh under his sleeves. He stopped. He walked back a step, then forward, and the cold was in the same place both times, exactly the same place, as though he were passing through a doorway that had been left standing in the middle of the hall."
    },
    {
      "line": 25,
      "text": "“It’s an old house. Old houses are cold. This one has been empty since March — it’s probably got the damp in its bones.” They grinned at him, that quick slanting grin that had been the first thing he’d ever liked about them. “You’ll be telling me it’s ghosts by Friday.”"
    },
    {
      "line": 33,
      "text": "He spent the afternoon on the windows. It was the one thing that bothered him enough to act on. From inside, the streaks looked worse — a film laid over the world, so that the lane outside was always slightly out of focus, always a little further away than it should be. He filled a bucket with hot water and vinegar, found a rag in the kitchen drawer under a nest of old keys, and went at the front room glass with his whole arm."
    },
    {
      "line": 49,
      "text": "It was a small sound, a single wooden knock high up near the landing, the noise a step makes when weight comes off it. Dave put his fork down. His partner was talking about the water pressure, about the boiler, about the neighbours they hadn’t met, and they did not pause. The stair knocked again, second step up this time, then a third, a slow descending series, as though someone had climbed halfway down in the night and thought better of it and gone back up."
    },
    {
      "line": 61,
      "text": "In the hallway, at the edge of the light from the kitchen door, something moved. Not a shape. A wrongness in the air, a thickness that passed across the far end of the runner and was gone before his eyes had finished turning. He stood with his hands in the sink and counted his own heartbeats until they slowed."
    },
    {
      "line": 67,
      "text": "In the morning it was a quarter-turn out from the wall, facing the door."
    },
    {
      "line": 83,
      "text": "He turned and went to find his coat, and he said nothing about the chair, and he did not look back at it, and he thought about it all the way down the lane past the cemetery, where the mist lay low even though the sun was out, and thickened along the oldest rows."
    }
  ]
}
